Now I can see the full picture. Key observations from Wally (Build 16):

**What Wally contains:**
- USB-A solar input → D1 (Schottky C35722) → **V50 rail**
- **INA219 at 0x45** on V50 rail shunt (R13, 0.05Ω) — added in Build 16 specifically to measure USB port current. This is what shows 60-80mA.
- **AP7365** 3.3V LDO (takes V50 → V33 for ESP32, CP2104, LoRa, RTC)
- **V50 and V33** both pass through J2/J3 headers up to Daffodil
- ESP32, LoRa, RTC are all on Wally — Daffodil is purely sensors + power management

**The problem this reveals:** The V50 from solar powers the AP7365 (ESP32+LoRa load) on Wally AND travels through the header to power MCP73123 on Daffodil. Both loads share the same 5V rail from the panel. The INA219 at 0x45 sees total current (ESP32 load + charger).

---

**For the CN3801 redesign, you have two clean options:**

**Option A — CN3801 stays on Daffodil (minimal change)**
Replace MCP73123 with CN3801, V50 from header as before. MPPT pin set to ~4.2V. Problem: MPPT sags V50 to 4.2V — the AP7365 on Wally needs V50 > ~3.5V to regulate V33, so it would still work (just barely). Not ideal.

**Option B — CN3801 moves to Wally (recommended)**
Solar → CN3801 (on Wally, right next to J1). CN3801 VBAT output wire goes up through a header pin to the battery on Daffodil. Only 1 new header pin needed — you have 20 pins on J2/J3, so there's likely a spare. Cleanest architecture: all solar/MPPT logic on Wally, battery+sensors stay on Daffodil.

Which header pins are currently unused on J2/J3? That determines if Option B fits without a connector change.

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
The BQ24650RVAR is a very solid chip, and it's confirmed in stock at JLCPCB (C53712). Here's an honest comparison vs CN3801 for your specific case:

---

## BQ24650RVAR — What it is

- **Synchronous buck controller** — high efficiency, but it is a **controller only**. It needs **external N-channel MOSFETs** (2×), external inductor, bootstrap cap, gate resistors, sense resistor. ~8-12 extra components on the Daffodil board.
- MPPT via `MPPSET` pin (resistor divider, very precise)
- Float voltage fully programmable via `VFB` divider → LiFePO4 3.6V is trivial to set
- Status pins STAT1/STAT2 and temperature sense TS
- Input: 2.1V–26V (5V USB solar works fine)
- Up to 10A charge current (way more than you need, but you'd set it to ~500mA via sense resistor)
- QFN-20 package — requires good PCB layout for the switching node

---

## CN3801 — The simpler alternative

- **Fully integrated** — internal switch, needs only inductor + a few caps + 2 resistors
- MPPT via similar resistor divider
- LiFePO4 native (3.6V fixed termination)
- Input: 4.5V–28V
- Up to ~3A
- SOP-8 — easier to layout, more forgiving

---

## Recommendation for Daffodil

**If Daffodil is a compact shield, use CN3801.** Half the BOM, simpler layout, the efficiency difference is negligible at 500mA from a 5V rail.

**Use BQ24650 if** you anticipate needing >2A charge current in a future revision (bigger panel, bigger battery), or if you want the STAT1/STAT2 and TS pins wired into the ESP32 for firmware visibility into charge state.

One thing both share: with V50 arriving from Wally through the header, the MPPT set point must stay above ~4.5V to keep the AP7365 working on Wally. Set MPPSET/MPPT divider for 4.5–4.7V, not the theoretical panel Vmpp of ~5V. You'll give up a little solar power but the Wally 3.3V rail stays stable.

Sources:
- [BQ24650RVAR at JLCPCB (C53712)](https://jlcpcb.com/partdetail/TexasInstruments-BQ24650RVAR/C53712)
- [BQ24650 datasheet (TI)](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/bq24650.pdf)
- [CN3801 at JLCPCB (C840499)](https://jlcpcb.com/partdetail/ShangHai_ConsonanceElec-CN3801/C840499)

--- CLAUDE ---
Good — 3C on a 600mAh cell = **1.8A max charge current**. I'll design the CN3801 circuit around that. Here are all the formulas and component values:

---

## CN3801 Circuit — Daffodil Rev 8

### Key formulas

| Parameter | Formula | Your value |
|-----------|---------|------------|
| Charge current | `ICHG = 120mV / RCS` | 1.8A → RCS = **68mΩ** |
| MPPT voltage | `VMPPT = 1.205 × (1 + R1/R2)` | 4.5V → R1=270k, R2=100k |
| Compensation | COM pin: 120Ω + 220nF in series to GND | fixed |
| Regulation voltage | Fixed internally | 3.625V (LiFePO4 ✓) |

---

### Component list

| Ref | Value | Function | JLCPCB suggestion |
|-----|-------|----------|--------------------|
| IC | CN3801 | Charger | **C840499** |
| L1 | 10µH, ≥3A sat. current, ≤200mΩ DCR | Buck inductor | e.g. SWPA6045S100MT |
| RCS | 68mΩ, 1W, 2010 pkg | Current sense | LVK20R068DER or similar |
| C_IN | 10µF, 10V, X5R ceramic | Input decoupling | 1210 pkg |
| C_IN2 | 100nF, 10V | Input bypass | 0402 pkg |
| C_BAT | 10µF, 10V, X5R ceramic | Output decoupling | 0805 pkg |
| R1 | 270kΩ | MPPT top | 0402 |
| R2 | 100kΩ | MPPT bottom | 0402 |
| R_COMP | 120Ω | COM compensation | 0402 |
| C_COMP | 220nF | COM compensation | 0402 |
| R_TEMP | 10kΩ | TEMP pin disable (simulates 25°C) | 0402 |
| R_STAT | 4.7kΩ | STAT LED current limit (optional) | 0402 |

---

### Connections (schematic description)

```
V50 (from Wally header)
  │
  ├─── C_IN (10µF) ─── GND
  ├─── C_IN2 (100nF) ── GND
  │
  └─── CN3801 [VIN]

CN3801 [SW] ──── L1 (10µH) ──── [CSP]
                                    │
                                   RCS (68mΩ)
                                    │
CN3801 [BAT] ──────────────────────┘──── Battery (+)
  │
  └─── C_BAT (10µF) ─── GND ──── Battery (-)

MPPT divider:
  V50 ─── R1 (270k) ─── [MPPT] ─── R2 (100k) ─── GND

COM compensation:
  [COM] ─── R_COMP (120Ω) ─── C_COMP (220nF) ─── GND

TEMP disable:
  [TEMP] ─── R_TEMP (10kΩ) ─── GND

STAT (optional charge LED):
  [STAT] ─── R_STAT (4.7kΩ) ─── LED ─── GND
```

---

### Important layout notes for KiCad

1. **Keep the SW–L1–RCS–BAT loop as tight as possible** — this is the high-frequency switching node and will radiate if traces are long
2. **C_IN as close as possible to VIN and GND pins** of IC
3. **GND fill under the IC** — CN3801 is SOP-8, thermal pad on the bottom
4. **RCS in 2010 footprint** — it will dissipate up to 0.22W at 1.8A, needs copper area for heat spreading
5. **MPPT divider**: connect R1 from VIN (before C_IN, after D1 on Daffodil) not from the SW node

---

### Why MPPT at 4.5V (not 4.7V)

The V50 rail that CN3801 sees also feeds the AP7365 3.3V regulator on Wally via the header. The AP7365 needs ≥3.8V input. Setting MPPT at 4.5V gives the AP7365 0.7V of clean headroom and still loads the solar panel well below its Voc.

---

One important question for the PCB: does the Daffodil currently have space for the inductor L1? The MCP73123 had no inductor — that's the main footprint addition. A SWPA6045 is 6.0×6.0mm, or you can go smaller with a 4×4mm part at slightly higher DCR.

Sources:
- [CN3801 at JLCPCB (C840499)](https://jlcpcb.com/partdetail/ShangHai_ConsonanceElec-CN3801/C840499)
- [CN3801 datasheet page 5 — formulas](https://www.alldatasheet.com/html-pdf/1133240/CONSONANCE/CN3801/3518/5/CN3801.html)
- [CN3801 Hiveeyes 3A reference design](https://community.hiveeyes.org/t/modified-3a-1s-lifepo4-solar-mppt-loader-based-on-cn3801/570)

--- CLAUDE ---
Good call — 1C = **600mA**. That simplifies everything: smaller sense resistor, smaller inductor, standard 0402/0603 footprints.

### Updated values for 600mA charge current

**RCS = 120mV / 0.6A = 200mΩ (0.2Ω)**

Use a standard **0.2Ω 0402 or 0603 resistor** — no special current-sense part needed, power dissipation is only 0.2 × 0.6² = **72mW**, well within a 0402's 100mW rating.

| Ref | Old (1.8A) | New (600mA) | Notes |
|-----|-----------|------------|-------|
| RCS | 68mΩ, 2010 | **0.2Ω (200mΩ), 0402** | Standard resistor, no special part |
| L1 | 10µH, ≥3A | **10µH, ≥1.5A sat.** | Much smaller/cheaper inductor |
| C_IN | 10µF 1210 | **10µF 0805** | Smaller cap fine at lower current |

Everything else stays the same:

| Ref | Value |
|-----|-------|
| R1 (MPPT top) | 270kΩ 0402 |
| R2 (MPPT bot) | 100kΩ 0402 |
| R_COMP | 120Ω 0402 |
| C_COMP | 220nF 0402 |
| R_TEMP | 10kΩ 0402 |
| C_BAT | 10µF 0805 |
| C_IN2 | 100nF 0402 |

For the inductor at 1.5A saturation, something like **CDRH4D28-100NC** (4.4×4.4mm, 10µH, 2.1A) from LCSC/JLCPCB fits on a compact board. Much easier to place on Daffodil than a 6×6mm part.

The 600mA also means the V50 traces on the Wally header carry at most 600mA (device load) + 600mA (charger) = **1.2A peak** only during simultaneous LoRa TX + active charging — and since LoRa TX and solar peak rarely coincide perfectly, real-world current through the header stays comfortably under 1A most of the time.

--- USER ---
what about the inductor already in the daffodil?ost 3.3uH C2047622

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
That's the ME2149 boost converter inductor — it's not suitable for the CN3801. Here's why:

**Ripple calculation with 3.3µH at CN3801's 300kHz:**

```
ΔI_L = (VIN - VBAT) × VBAT / (L × VIN × f)
     = (4.7 - 3.6) × 3.6 / (3.3µH × 4.7 × 300kHz)
     = 3.96 / 4.65
     = 850mA ripple on 600mA average
```

That's **142% ripple** — far too high. Peak inductor current hits 1025mA. The IC would work but with excessive stress, high EMI, and poor efficiency.

The 3.3µH is sized for ME2149's switching frequency (typically 1.5–2MHz), not 300kHz. Keep it where it is.

**For CN3801 you need a separate inductor. At 300kHz targeting 30% ripple:**

```
L = (VIN - VBAT) × VBAT / (ΔI_ripple × VIN × f)
  = 1.1 × 3.6 / (0.18 × 4.7 × 300kHz)
  = 3.96 / 253,800
  = 15.6µH
```

Nearest standard value: **22µH**. At 30% ripple that gives comfortable headroom.

| Ref | Value | Rating | Footprint | Suggestion |
|-----|-------|--------|-----------|------------|
| L1 (CN3801) | **22µH** | ≥1.5A sat, ≤500mΩ DCR | 4040 or 5040 | e.g. CDRH5D28-220 |
| L_boost (ME2149) | 3.3µH (C2047622) | unchanged | unchanged | keep as-is |

A 22µH in a 5.0×5.0mm CDRH5D28 package is the smallest practical option. If board space on Daffodil is very tight, dropping to 10µH gives ~75% ripple which is workable but not ideal — the output cap (C_BAT) would need to increase to 22µF to filter it.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Root cause of 60-80mA solar limit**: Not the panel (three identical panels all give same result). Root cause is shared V50 rail — Wally device load (60-90mA for ESP32+LoRa) consumes all solar current, leaving nothing for MCP73123 to charge battery. MCP73123 only charges meaningfully during deep sleep when device load drops to µA.
   - **Why MCP73123 can't MPPT**: Linear charger, no switching, no input current regulation. CN3801 fix: actively regulates VIN to 4.46V MPPT setpoint, drawing maximum current from panel within the rail voltage constraints.
   - **Inductor reuse question resolved**: 3.3µH (ME2149 inductor) gives 850mA p-p ripple at CN3801's 300kHz → 142% ripple on 600mA average. Must use separate 22µH inductor for CN3801. Keep 3.3µH for ME2149 boost.
   - **MPPT voltage constraint**: AP7365 on Wally needs V50 ≥ 3.8V. Set MPPT at 4.5V (not lower) to maintain 0.7V headroom for the 3.3V LDO.
   - **Chat export**: JSONL structure has type=user (content as string) and type=assistant (content as list of typed items, need type='text'). Deduplication by uuid prevents duplicate messages from being exported.

   The session concluded with the user saying "ok i m going to stop now i will continue tomorrow" after the CN3801 circuit design was completed and the chat export functionality was set up.

   The last technical work was the inductor analysis: determining that the existing 3.3µH inductor (C2047622, used for ME2149 boost converter) cannot be reused for CN3801 at 300kHz switching frequency because it produces 850mA ripple current on a 600mA average — 142% ripple, far too high. The required value is 22µH for ~30% ripple.

   Complete CN3801 circuit specification (for Daffodil Rev 8, replacing MCP73123):
   - IC: CN3801 (JLCPCB C840499)
   - RCS: 0.2Ω 0402 (600mA charge current)
   - L1: 22µH, ≥1.5A saturation current, separate new part
   - R1: 270kΩ 0402 (MPPT top)
   - R2: 100kΩ 0402 (MPPT bot) → VMPPT = 4.46V
   - R_COMP: 120Ω 0402 (COM pin)
   - C_COMP: 220nF 0402 (COM pin)
   - R_TEMP: 10kΩ 0402 (TEMP disable)
   - C_IN: 10µF 0805 10V X5R
   - C_IN2: 100nF 0402
   - C_BAT: 10µF 0805 10V X5R

Now I can see the schematic. Let me address both questions:

**1. MCP73123 STAT pin**

The STAT pin is an open-drain active-low output:
- Pulls LOW while charging is in progress
- Goes Hi-Z (floating) when charge is complete, in standby, or fault

Without a pull-up resistor to VCC, STAT has no function — you can't drive an LED, you can't monitor it, and you can't use it to control anything else. It's not harmful to leave it floating if you're genuinely not using it.

**2. Why the battery is discharging at 2:30pm with full sun**

Looking at the schematic, the `DEVICE_POWER` section in the bottom right contains a MOSFET that controls whether the ME2149 boost converter (battery → V50) is active. Here's the problem:

The boost converter and the MCP73123 charger are **both connected to V50 simultaneously**. When solar is present:
- Solar provides V50 at ~4.7–5V
- MCP73123 draws from V50 to charge the battery ✓
- **But ME2149 is still active**, drawing current from the battery and pushing it back up to V50

So the battery is being charged by MCP73123 at the same time ME2149 is draining it. They're fighting each other in a loop. The net result is small positive or negative current readings depending on which one is "winning" at each sample — exactly what you see in the 2:30pm data (alternating +28mA, -52mA, +28mA).

The MOSFET (Q4) is supposed to disable ME2149 when solar is active, but if its gate signal is not properly derived from a reliable solar-present indicator, it won't switch off cleanly. Can you tell me what signal is driving Q4's gate in the actual PCB?

--- USER ---
q4 is being control by V50_I which comes from the header pin 9 of j1

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
That's the root of the problem. Here's why it doesn't work:

**V50_I cannot distinguish between "solar is providing power" and "ME2149 is providing power"**

At night with no solar:
- Battery → ME2149 → V50 (5V) → flows out through J1 pin 9 back to Wally
- So V50_I = ~5V, coming **from** ME2149

During the day with solar:
- Solar → Wally → J1 pin 9 → V50_I = ~5V, coming **from the panel**

In both cases Q4's gate sees ~5V. It has no way to know which source is driving V50_I, so it can't reliably switch off ME2149 when solar is present. The two situations are electrically identical at pin 9.

**What's happening at 2:30pm:**
Q4 is either always on or always off — either way ME2149 and MCP73123 are both active simultaneously, fighting each other through V50. That's why you see the small alternating ±current readings in the data.

**The fix for Rev 8:**

To properly detect "solar present", you need a signal that only exists when power is coming **in** from Wally, not going out. Two clean options:

1. **Use the INA219 at 0x45 on Wally** — it already measures USB port current (added in Build 16). If current is positive, solar is present. Read it over I2C and use it in firmware to enable/disable ME2149 via GPIO instead of using V50_I directly.

2. **Add a comparator on the solar panel voltage directly** — before the Schottky diode on Wally, the panel voltage is higher than V50 only when the panel is actually producing. A simple voltage divider + comparator output to Daffodil via a spare header pin would give you a clean solar-present signal.

Option 1 is free since the hardware already exists — you already read the INA219 at 0x41 (battery), just add reads of 0x45 and use that current reading to gate ME2149 in firmware.

--- CLAUDE ---
Right, so in the current hardware this exact flaw is live. The Q4 circuit can't distinguish solar from ME2149, so in practice ME2149 is likely always running — draining the battery into V50 at the same time MCP73123 is trying to charge it from V50. They fight each other continuously.

That explains the 2:30pm data perfectly. Looking at what you had around that time:

- 14:15 → 14:43: steady **+28mA** (battery discharging)
- 14:10: **+136mA** spike (heavy discharge)
- 14:12: brief **-52mA** (MCP73123 briefly winning)

The solar panel is producing current, MCP73123 is putting some of it into the battery, but ME2149 is simultaneously pulling it back out. The net result is the battery slowly drains through the afternoon even with full sun — which is exactly what you observed.

This is also why the battery has been dying repeatedly. Every sunny afternoon the battery should be recovering, but it can't because ME2149 is working against the charger all day.

The CN3801 replacement fixes the charger side, but you'll still need to fix the Q4 control signal in Rev 8 to properly disable ME2149 when solar is present — otherwise you'll have the same fight with a more powerful charger.

--- USER ---
if i had the current sensor at 0x45 and i knew there was solar panel power, how would i turn off the me2149

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
The ME2149 has an EN (enable) pin — pull it LOW and the boost converter shuts off.

**In the current Rev 6 circuit**, EN is controlled by Q4 driven by V50_I, which as we established can't detect solar reliably.

**For Rev 8**, the change is:
- Remove the V50_I → Q4 gate connection
- Wire ME2149 EN directly to a free ESP32 GPIO pin (through the Wally header or on Daffodil itself)

Then in firmware:

```cpp
// On each clock tick, after reading INA219 at 0x45:
float solarCurrent = ina219_0x45.getCurrent_mA();

if (solarCurrent > 20.0) {        // solar is providing power
    digitalWrite(PIN_ME2149_EN, LOW);   // disable boost — let solar run the bus
} else {
    digitalWrite(PIN_ME2149_EN, HIGH);  // enable boost — battery must supply V50
}
```

The 20mA threshold gives you noise margin — the INA219 has a few mA of offset, and you want to be sure the panel is actually producing before cutting off battery backup.

**One edge case to handle**: if a cloud passes and solar drops suddenly, ME2149 re-enables within one clock tick (1 second), but V50 could sag in that window. You could add hysteresis — only disable ME2149 if solar current has been > 20mA for 3 consecutive seconds, and only re-enable if it drops below 5mA for 3 seconds.

What free GPIO pins do you have available on the Rev 8 layout?

i need some sql to change all the telepathons of name Top Tank that have the device id of pffodil rename them to Daffodil

--- CLAUDE ---

--- CLAUDE ---
The database is on chinampamonitor, not local. Here's the SQL to run there — it loops through all telepathon tables automatically:

```sql
DO $$
DECLARE
    t text;
    rows_updated int;
BEGIN
    FOR t IN
        SELECT table_name
        FROM information_schema.tables
        WHERE table_name LIKE 'telepathon_%'
        AND table_schema = 'public'
        ORDER BY table_name
    LOOP
        EXECUTE format(
            'UPDATE %I
             SET data = replace(data, ''pffodil'', ''Daffodil'')
             WHERE TRIM(data::jsonb->>''Name'') = ''TopTank''
             AND data LIKE ''%%pffodil%%''',
            t
        );
        GET DIAGNOSTICS rows_updated = ROW_COUNT;
        IF rows_updated > 0 THEN
            RAISE NOTICE 'Table %: % rows updated', t, rows_updated;
        END IF;
    END LOOP;
END $$;
```

It uses a simple text `replace()` on the raw `data` column — safe because `pffodil` is unique enough that it won't accidentally match anything else. The `RAISE NOTICE` lines will tell you which tables had records fixed and how many.

Run it on chinampamonitor with:
```bash
psql -d teleonome -c "<paste sql here>"
```

Or if you want to **preview first** without changing anything:

```sql
SELECT table_name,
       count(*) AS records_to_fix
FROM information_schema.tables
CROSS JOIN LATERAL (
    SELECT 1
    FROM teleonome.public.telepathon_2026_6_23  -- example
    WHERE TRIM(data::jsonb->>'Name') = 'TopTank'
    AND data LIKE '%pffodil%'
) sub
WHERE table_name LIKE 'telepathon_%';
```

Actually the preview query is harder to generalise — just run the DO block, the NOTICE output will tell you exactly what got changed.

The `data` column is `jsonb` type — need to cast to `text` for `LIKE` and `replace`, then cast back. Fix:

```sql
DO $$
DECLARE
    t text;
    rows_updated int;
BEGIN
    FOR t IN
        SELECT table_name
        FROM information_schema.tables
        WHERE table_name LIKE 'telepathon_%'
        AND table_schema = 'public'
        ORDER BY table_name
    LOOP
        EXECUTE format(
            'UPDATE %I
             SET data = replace(data::text, ''pffodil'', ''Daffodil'')::jsonb
             WHERE TRIM(data->>''Name'') = ''TopTank''
             AND data::text LIKE ''%%pffodil%%''',
            t
        );
        GET DIAGNOSTICS rows_updated = ROW_COUNT;
        IF rows_updated > 0 THEN
            RAISE NOTICE 'Table %: % rows updated', t, rows_updated;
        END IF;
    END LOOP;
END $$;
```

Two changes from before:
- `replace(data::text, ..., ...)::jsonb` — cast to text for replace, cast result back to jsonb
- `data::text LIKE '%%pffodil%%'` — cast to text for LIKE
- `data->>'Name'` — removed redundant `::jsonb` cast since column is already jsonb

Here's the picture for today 07:18–13:34 AEST:

**Battery voltage — big anomaly early morning**
The battery voltage reads **0.75V from 07:18 to 09:01** — that's clearly wrong for a LiFePO4 cell (should be 3.0–3.6V). Either the INA219 lost its calibration after a deep sleep or the battery was completely dead and the device was running off a residual charge. **Current state (13:34)**

The spotty Pi is masking what's actually happening on the device. The 0.75V readings early morning are particularly suspicious — was the device rebooted around 09:30?

--- USER ---
the .75v reading in the morinng is because the battery is dead i repaced the battery at about 89:30 so ignore thhe .75v, I am using Paula to receive data and i kept an eye on itt, and icould see that one lora transmission the current would be -130 but  then the next one will be 59, it was sunny all day and i ahave a voltmeter in line with the device, i could see that the voltage has never been below 5.6V coming into the device, why is the device charging one moment then discharging the battery the next moment

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
That alternating pattern is exactly the ME2149 vs MCP73123 fight, and the LoRa transmission is exposing it on every cycle.

Here's what's happening beat by beat:

**Between transmissions (idle):**
- Solar provides 5.6V on V50
- MCP73123 charges battery → INA219 reads **-130mA** ✓

**During LoRa transmission:**
- LoRa TX pulls ~120mA spike from V50
- V50 sags momentarily under that load
- ME2149 sees the sag and kicks in, pulling current from the battery to prop up V50
- INA219 reads **+59mA** (battery discharging into ME2149)
- LoRa finishes, V50 recovers, MCP73123 resumes charging

So you get: charge → transmit → discharge → charge → transmit → discharge, perfectly in sync with the LoRa cadence.

The root cause is still the same — ME2149 is never properly disabled because Q4's control signal (V50_I) can't tell the difference between "solar is holding V50 up" and "V50 is sagging, boost it". The moment V50 dips even 100–200mV during the LoRa spike, ME2149 interprets it as "no solar, I need to help" and fires.

**Net effect on the battery:** It's not a wash. MCP73123 charges at -130mA for maybe 8 seconds, then ME2149 discharges at +59mA for 1–2 seconds of LoRa TX. The battery is making net progress charging, but it's very inefficient — the ME2149 is burning solar energy in a boost conversion loop that's completely unnecessary.

This won't be fixable in the current hardware without cutting the Q4 gate signal and tying ME2149 EN directly to a GPIO. Until Rev 8, the battery will charge slowly despite good sun.

--- USER ---
what if we were too turn off the leds when sending lora? the lora logo is 4 leds

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
It would help a little but probably not solve it completely. Here's why:

**The LED saving:**
4 WS2812B LEDs at your current brightness level (20% from the data) draw roughly 10–12mA each, so about 40–48mA total. Turning them off during LoRa TX removes that from V50's load.

**The LoRa TX load:**
ESP32 during active transmission pulls ~80–100mA, plus the SX127x at 5dBm adds another ~20–28mA. Total LoRa TX load is ~120–130mA regardless of LEDs.

**Why it might not be enough:**
The V50 sag that triggers ME2149 is caused mainly by the ESP32+LoRa spike, not the LEDs. Removing 40mA from a 170mA total load reduces the sag by about 25% — it might shift ME2149 from triggering every cycle to triggering every second or third cycle, but it won't stop it consistently.

**However**, there is a secondary benefit — right now the display state during LoRa TX is showing the LoRa logo (4 LEDs). If you turn them off for the ~100–200ms of actual TX, you also reduce heat, LED wear, and it costs nothing in user experience since 100ms is imperceptible.

The real lever is not LEDs, it's that ME2149 output voltage and the solar V50 are too close together. If you could raise ME2149's output setpoint slightly above what solar typically provides, ME2149 would never compete. But that risks other things so it's not a clean fix either.

The proper fix is still Rev 8 — CN3801 + GPIO-controlled ME2149.

You're right, apologies. LiFePO4 has an extremely flat discharge curve — voltage tells you almost nothing between ~10% and ~90% SOC. At 3.21V under a 154mA load it could genuinely be anywhere from 15% to 60%.

The only reliable indicators for LiFePO4 SOC are:
- **Coulomb counting** (integrating current over time) — not implemented yet
- **Voltage only works at the extremes**: below ~3.0V = nearly dead, above ~3.55V at rest = near full

What I can say with confidence:
- **Not full** — it never reached the 3.6V MCP73123 charge termination voltage today
- **Not critically low yet** — 3.21V under 154mA load isn't in the danger zone
- **Trend is the real concern** — sun is gone (51 lux), drawing 154mA, and it didn't charge well today due to the ME2149 fight

The estimated runtime of 1.56 hrs from the firmware is calculated from voltage-derived SOC, so that number is also unreliable for the same reason. Don't rely on it.

remember the script wherei asked you to change the records with pffodil? now i need the same but replace the records with device id of Daffodil to daffodilTF

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Same script, just different strings. Note that "Daffodil" appears in both the DeneWords value AND inside the Raw Data string (`#Daffodil#`), so a plain text replace catches both in one shot:

```sql
DO $$
DECLARE
    t text;
    rows_updated int;
BEGIN
    FOR t IN
        SELECT table_name
        FROM information_schema.tables
        WHERE table_name LIKE 'telepathon_%'
        AND table_schema = 'public'
        ORDER BY table_name
    LOOP
        EXECUTE format(
            'UPDATE %I
             SET data = replace(data::text, ''Daffodil'', ''daffodilTF'')::jsonb
             WHERE TRIM(data->>''Name'') = ''TopTank''
             AND data::text LIKE ''%%Daffodil%%''',
            t
        );
        GET DIAGNOSTICS rows_updated = ROW_COUNT;
        IF rows_updated > 0 THEN
            RAISE NOTICE 'Table %: % rows updated', t, rows_updated;
        END IF;
    END LOOP;
END $$;
```

One thing to be aware of: if any other field in the TopTank records contains the word "Daffodil" (e.g. a sensor name or description), it will get renamed too. From the data structure we've seen that's unlikely, but worth keeping in mind.

--- USER ---
what if there are records that contains the word daffodilTF

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
You're safe — PostgreSQL's `replace()` is case-sensitive. We're searching for `'Daffodil'` (capital D), and `'daffodilTF'` starts with a lowercase `d`, so it won't match. Running the script on a record that already has `daffodilTF` does nothing — there's no `Daffodil` to find in it.

The `AND data::text LIKE '%Daffodil%'` WHERE clause also means those already-correct records are skipped entirely and never touched.

--- CLAUDE ---
So the TPL5010 should have fired **5 times** in 82 minutes. The device is rebooting every 15 minutes but never gets far enough to send a LoRa packet.

That points to a boot-loop: the ESP32 starts up, something fails (likely the battery sags the moment it draws current to initialize), and it crashes before `sendMessage()` is ever called. Watchdog resets it. Repeat.

The last two records show essentially **zero charging current** at 5859 lux — that's the real problem. The MCP73123 should be pushing 60+ mA into the battery at that light level, but it's doing nothing. A few possibilities:

1. **Battery is in deep discharge** — MCP73123 has a preconditioning threshold (~2.5V for LiFePO4 variants). If the cell dropped below it during a previous sleep, the charger goes into trickle/fault mode and barely touches it.

2. **Battery has failed internally** — high internal resistance means it can't accept current. Voltage reads 3.26V open-circuit but sags the moment you load it.

3. **V50_I signal is blocking Q4** — same circuit issue we discussed. If the charger's VBAT sense path is seeing something wrong.

**Fastest diagnostic**: swap the battery. You've replaced it before — if a fresh cell brings the device back immediately, the battery has failed again (6th one). If it still doesn't boot with a fresh battery, then the circuit is the problem.

That's completely fair. A fresh battery dead in one day with full sun is not a battery problem — it's a charging problem. Swapping again just gives you the same result tomorrow.

During the day when the ME2149 is actively holding V50 up to serve the load, the MCP73123 sees the rail already at voltage and backs off. The battery never gets a proper charge cycle. By nightfall the battery is already low, and overnight it runs it flat.

--- USER ---
so if i connect gpio18 instead of q4 how do i know when to turn it on and off? and what happens when the esp32 goes to sleep?

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Two separate problems:

**How do you know when to switch it?**

You'd read the INA219 current on each wake cycle. The logic is:
- If battery current is charging (negative) and magnitude is meaningful (say > 20mA) → solar is doing the job → disable ME2149, let MCP73123 charge
- If current flips positive (discharging) → solar is gone or insufficient → re-enable ME2149

You could also use the BH1750 lux reading as a cross-check — under a threshold it's night, don't bother checking current.

**What happens during sleep?**

The RTC and TPL5010 still need V50 during deep sleep. If ME2149 is off, V50 has no source and everything dies — including the watchdog that's supposed to wake you back up. So you **must** hold ME2149 enabled during sleep with `gpio_hold_en()`, same as you already do for pins 23 and 26.

The pattern would be: wake up → check INA219 → if solar present, disable ME2149 for the wake period → before sleep, re-enable ME2149 → `gpio_hold_en()` → `esp_deep_sleep_start()`.

**On GPIO 18** — that's the ultrasonic/flow meter pin. If TopTank isn't using sonar it's technically free, but it's a shared-purpose pin. GPIO 32 was what we identified as clean and available for this in Rev 8. Worth using that instead to avoid conflicts if you ever change modes.

--- USER ---
but in wally build 16vi will have ina219 on the usb so i could know if there is voltage at the panel and then what?

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
With a solar-side INA219 you'd have direct, reliable detection — no more inferring from battery current. If solar voltage > threshold (say 4.5V) and current > threshold (say 30mA), solar is genuinely producing.

Then the logic on each Daffodil wake cycle:
- Read solar INA219 → solar present → disable ME2149, MCP73123 charges cleanly
- Solar gone → enable ME2149, it boosts battery to V50

But there's still the sleep problem: Daffodil sleeps and can't read the INA219. ME2149 has to be held ON during sleep regardless, because that's what keeps V50 alive for the RTC and TPL5010.

The deeper question is **who controls the ME2149 during sleep**. Two options:

1. **Daffodil holds GPIO high** before sleeping → ME2149 stays on → wastes some battery if sun is shining while it sleeps, but safe. On wakeup, immediately check solar INA219 and disable if sun is up.

2. **Wally controls ME2149** — if Wally is always-on or has its own INA219, Wally holds the ME2149 signal and manages it continuously, even while Daffodil sleeps. Daffodil just follows.

Option 2 is cleaner architecturally — Wally sees solar directly and owns the power rail decision. But it depends on whether Wally is always awake and whether there's a signal line between them.

Is Wally always on, or does it also sleep?

--- USER ---
well, its pretty simple, because the daffodil is the device that is made of wally and the daffodil shield, it has a rtc so it knowa what time and what date and it has latitude so it knows where it is, it also knows what time sunset is so itknows tht at night it has to keep the battery on

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Exactly — SolarInfo already gives you sunrise and sunset. The logic is simple:

- **Daytime** (between sunrise and sunset): disable ME2149, solar feeds V50 directly, MCP73123 charges the battery unimpeded
- **Nighttime**: enable ME2149, battery boosts to V50, device runs as it does now
- **Before sleep**: hold the GPIO in whatever state matches the current time window via `gpio_hold_en()` — so it stays correct while the ESP32 is asleep

The INA219 on the Wally USB side becomes the safety net for one edge case: **overcast days**. If it's "daytime" by the clock but the solar panel is producing less than the load needs, ME2149 is off and V50 will sag — the device could brownout. With the solar INA219 you can catch that: if daytime but solar current is below a threshold, re-enable ME2149 and run from battery instead.

So the full decision tree on each wake:
1. Is it night? → ME2149 on
2. Is it day and solar INA219 > threshold? → ME2149 off, charge the battery
3. Is it day but solar INA219 < threshold (overcast)? → ME2149 on, run from battery

Clean, reliable, and all the ingredients are already there — SolarInfo, RTC, and the INA219 you're adding in build 16.

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
That table explains everything. Here's what's happening:

**Why LEDs are dim/off and transmission is slow:**

The `isLoraTxSafe()` function multiplies LED brightness by `efficiency`. In Melbourne in winter, the sun is low enough that efficiency only crosses the **0.3 threshold at 09:30 AEST**. Before that, the firmware treats it like night:

| Window | Efficiency | Sleep | LED brightness |
|--------|-----------|-------|---------------|
| 08:00–09:30 | 0.13–0.28 | ~280s | 255 × 0.20 ≈ **53/255 (dim)** |
| 09:30–15:00 | 0.34–0.49 | 60s | 255 × 0.44 ≈ **111/255 (on)** |
| 15:00–sunset | 0.28–0.00 | ~390s | **dim again** |

**The bug**: after sunrise, when efficiency is still < 0.3, `calculateOptimalSleepTime()` calculates `minutesToSunrise` as negative (you're past sunrise), then wraps it to ~1380 minutes (next day). The night formula then gives ~280s sleep instead of the 60s you'd get in proper daytime. So between 08:00 and 09:30 the device wakes every 4.5 minutes instead of every minute.

You saw that LoRa packet because you were watching around 09:30 when it just crossed the threshold and switched to 60s sleep. Right now at 10:30 efficiency is 0.44, so it should be transmitting every 60 seconds and LEDs should be on at about half brightness.

The effective solar day for this device in Melbourne winter is only **09:30 to 15:00 — 5.5 hours**, not the full ~9.5 hours between sunrise and sunset.

--- USER ---
but the panel has had voltage over 5.6 for almost an hour, why not send more often

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Because `calculateOptimalSleepTime()` never looks at V50 voltage. It makes its decision purely from the astronomical efficiency calculation — sun angle, time of day, day of year. Actual measured voltage on the rail plays no part.

So even with 5.92V on V50 and real power flowing in, if the geometric efficiency is < 0.3 (sun still too low in the sky), the function takes the night path and sleeps ~280s. The two systems are completely decoupled — the power is there but the sleep logic doesn't know it.

The fix would be straightforward: in `calculateOptimalSleepTime()`, check V50 voltage first. If V50 is above, say, 5.4V, the panel is clearly producing — skip the efficiency gate and return 60s regardless. But that's the same as what you'd get from just using the solar INA219 on Wally build 16 as the trigger.

--- USER ---
so right now wit wally 15 and no usb in219 what can i do?

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
You already have what you need. `getCapacitorVoltage()` reads V50 from ADS1115 channel 3 — that's the 5.92V you're measuring. The fix is one extra check at the top of `calculateOptimalSleepTime()` in PowerManager.cpp:

```cpp
// If V50 is high, solar is live — short sleep regardless of sun angle
if (getCapacitorVoltage() > 5.4f) {
    return 60UL;
}
```

No hardware changes. V50 above 5.4V means the panel is genuinely pushing power onto the rail, so there's no reason to wait for the geometry to catch up. Device sleeps 60s instead of 280s from the moment the panel wakes up, not from 09:30 when the math agrees the sun is high enough.

The threshold of 5.4V is safe because the ME2149 boost output is nominally 5.0–5.2V — if V50 is above that, solar must be supplementing it. Want me to make that change?

You're right, that idea doesn't work. V50 is always above 5V whether it's solar or ME2149 — indistinguishable.

But you have two other options with no hardware changes:

**Option 1 — Use actual sunrise/sunset (cleanest fix)**  
`dailySolarData.sunrise` and `dailySolarData.sunset` from Dusk2Dawn are already calculated accurately. Right now the daytime gate is `efficiency > 0.3` which shrinks your effective day to 09:30–15:00. Just replace it with a proper time window check — if current time is between sunrise and sunset, sleep 60s. That gives you the full 07:32–17:09 today.

**Option 2 — Use the BH1750 lux reading**  
The light sensor is already being read. If lux > some threshold (say 200–500 lux), it's clearly daylight regardless of sun angle. Pass that into `calculateOptimalSleepTime()` as an additional condition. Bonus: it also handles real-world clouds naturally, whereas the astronomical calculation assumes clear sky.

Option 1 requires changing only PowerManager.cpp. Option 2 requires passing lux in from Daffodil.ino but gives you actual observed light rather than theoretical. Do you want me to implement either?

--- CLAUDE ---
Found the LED gate. And I think I see both problems. Let me lay it out:

**LEDs off** — controlled by `minimumEfficiencyForLed` (line 2390). Default is 40% from NVS. At 10:30 AEST today efficiency is 43.7% which should just barely clear it. But there's a critical question: **did the RTC lose power when the battery died?**

If the PCF8563T reset to a wrong time, the device thinks it's, say, 08:00 — efficiency 12.7% — well below 40%. LEDs stay off, `isSleepMode = true` (line 2480), the device wakes, sends one LoRa packet, and goes back to sleep without ever turning LEDs on.

**LoRa transmitting but not received** — also explained by this. The TOTP code in every packet is time-based. If the RTC reset and the time is wrong, the TOTP is wrong. The receiving device gets the LoRa packet fine at the physical layer but **rejects it at authentication**. Both receivers see nothing valid.

The 0.17A spike proves the SX127x is physically transmitting. The two receivers not getting it points squarely at TOTP rejection, which points at a bad RTC time.

**Quick check**: connect serial (115200 baud) and type `printCurrentDSDData`. That'll show you what time the RTC thinks it is right now. If it's wrong, `SetTime#...` will fix everything — LEDs, LoRa, sleep, all of it.

--- USER ---
yes it was one hour late it had 10:01 and is now 10:59,

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
That's exactly it — 1 hour slow. At RTC time 10:01, efficiency is only 27.9% which is below the 40% LED threshold. The TOTP is also generating wrong codes because the time is off.

The 1 hour is almost certainly the daylight saving time shift — the RTC was set during AEDT (summer, UTC+11) but the battery failure caused a reset and it came back without the DST offset, putting it on AEST (UTC+10). In June there's no DST so it should be AEST, but the original set time was probably AEDT.

You need to fix the RTC time via serial. What's the exact SetTime format in the firmware?

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
From the examples in the code, the format is `SetTime#DD#M#YY#DOW#HH#MM#SS`.

Today is Saturday June 27, 2026. Day of week = 6 (Saturday, where 0=Sunday). Send this via serial at the exact moment you type it:

```
SetTime#27#6#26#6#11#05#00
```

Replace `11#05#00` with the actual current HH#MM#SS when you send it. Once the RTC is corrected:
- TOTP codes will be valid → LoRa packets will be accepted by both receivers
- Efficiency at real ~11:00 is 46.8% > 40% threshold → LEDs turn on
- Sleep time drops to 60s

--- USER ---

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
You're right. During deep sleep the total draw is only 2–5mA for the RTC and TPL5010. The ME2149 supplies that tiny current from the battery without pulling V50 below the solar panel's voltage, so the panel has nowhere to push current — inline meter reads zero and the battery drains uninterrupted. The MCP73123 isn't charging during sleep because ME2149 is holding V50 above the threshold where it would need to contribute.

So the battery has been draining continuously all day — wake or sleep — with essentially zero net charging. By 11:23 it's almost certainly below `minimumLEDVoltage = 3.18V`, which is why LEDs are still off: the efficiency check at line 2390 passes (47% > 40%), briefly turns LED_CONTROL HIGH, but line 2502 immediately overrides it because battery is below 3.18V and turns it back LOW.

For the LoRa not being received — did you actually send the `SetTime` command via serial, or are you saying the wall clock shows 11:23 and the device RTC was still 1 hour behind? Because if the RTC is still at ~10:25, TOTP codes are still wrong.

The bigger picture: this battery is done for today. The charging circuit isn't working and the device is running purely on discharge. Without swapping the battery or fixing the hardware, the device will keep hitting these thresholds and eventually stop waking entirely.

That data tells the story clearly. Almost every reading is near-zero charging (-0.04 to -0.40mA), with one interesting spike at -238mA at 10:22. That spike proves the MCP73123 **can** charge at full rate — it briefly got the chance before ME2149 stabilised and took V50 back, then the charger backed off to zero again.

**With Wally 15 as-is, there's no pure software fix for the charging problem.** The ME2149 will always be competing with the MCP73123 on V50. No firmware change changes that — it's wired that way.

The one thing you can do without making a PCB is **run a wire from a free GPIO to the ME2149 enable pin**. You don't need the Rev 8 board — a thin wire soldered to the enable pin and a GPIO header pin is enough. You already have the firmware logic in your head: daytime (sunrise to sunset) → pull EN low → ME2149 off → solar holds V50 → MCP73123 charges freely. Night → pull EN high → ME2149 back on → battery powers V50.

Which GPIO is accessible on the Wally 15 header and whether the ME2149 EN pin is reachable on that board will determine if this is practical. Do you have easy physical access to the ME2149 and a free header pin on Wally 15?

The -238mA spike at 10:22 also tells you the MCP73123 and battery are both fine — the charger is capable, it just never gets the space to run.

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Good. So time is correct, battery is 3.34V which is above all the voltage cutoffs. LEDs should be turning on — but the wake window is only about 3–5 seconds every 60 seconds. You'd need to be watching it at exactly the right moment. They're not staying on because the device runs its tick, transmits, and goes back to sleep. It's not a display you'd easily catch standing outside.

The bigger unresolved issue is LoRa not being received despite correct time. The 0.17A spike confirms the SX127x is active, but **active doesn't mean transmitting** — `performCAD()` runs before every TX and if it sees the channel as busy it aborts. The device still draws full current during the CAD listen window. So you could be seeing the active draw without an actual packet going out.

The quickest way to know what's really happening is USB serial. Connect and either:
- Enable debug with `debug#1` — it prints the CAD result, LoRa TX outcome, efficiency, battery voltage every tick
- Or just type `printCurrentDSDData` to see the current state snapshot

That will show you immediately whether LoRa TX is being skipped by CAD, what efficiency and battery readings it's seeing, and whether anything else is blocking it.

Found the problem immediately. Look at line 2:


**Year 2926, not 2026.** The first SetTime had a typo — the TOTP codes were being generated for 900 years in the future. That's why both receivers rejected every packet. The last line confirms you fixed it:

That one is correct. LoRa should work now.

A few other things from this output worth noting:

**LEDs were actually on** — `ledBrightness=255 digitalRead(LED_CONTROL)=1` at displayStatus 0, 1, and 2. You were probably missing them because the wake window in the field is only ~3-4 seconds per 60-second cycle.

**The INA219 bus voltage is reading 0.75–0.98V** which is way too low for a LiFePO4 cell. The current readings (-0.2 to -0.4mA) are essentially zero/noise. Something is wrong with how the INA219 is connected or calibrated — it's not giving useful battery voltage data.

**The RTC coin cell is unstable** — `rtcBatVolt` jumping between 4.84, 2.76, and -1. That's why the RTC loses time every time the main battery dies. The coin cell isn't backing it up reliably. Worth replacing the coin cell.

Also: `sunrise=513` = 08:33, but real Melbourne sunrise today is ~07:32. The Dusk2Dawn call has DST applied year-round (`true`), adding a phantom hour to sunrise/sunset even in winter.

This is much more interesting than the earlier sparse data. Let me analyze the full picture:

--- CLAUDE ---
Clear picture. Two very different phases in this data:

**10:20–11:17 (wrong RTC year, 270s sleep)**: almost no battery activity. The device slept 4.5 minutes between each wake. Tiny near-zero current most of the time. Battery just coasting.

**11:25–11:33 (correct time, 60s sleep, full mode)**: dramatic change. The MCP73123 IS charging at -99 to -238mA in short bursts, but ME2149 immediately pulls it back out during the active wake period. Average charging spike is **-110mA**, but there are **twice as many discharge samples** at 45mA average — so the charging is brief and the discharging is sustained.

The battery voltage tells the truth: **3.30–3.34V the entire time, a 40mV range, flat**. All that charging and discharging is the two circuits fighting each other in a loop. Solar energy is churning through the system but the battery isn't accumulating any of it.

The critical implication: **during the solar day the battery is maintained at roughly this level** — not improving, but not getting worse either. What kills it is the night. Once solar stops, there's nothing for MCP73123 to work with, ME2149 runs purely from battery for sleep current and all the night wake cycles, and 14.5 hours of drain with zero recovery kills a LiFePO4 by morning.

A new battery will behave the same way — same flat daytime, same overnight drain. Without the ME2149 EN control wired up, this pattern doesn't change.

ok so for my own sake, tell me exacty what do  hardware wise, from where i see it i need to remove r17 and q4 and connect pin 1 ce of me2191 to gpio 18 and then in the software turn off 18 when the voltage either at the new ina219 in line with the usb port or using the ads1115 because i am also changing that from v50 to v50_I which is what comes in from the header

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Your plan is correct. Here's exactly what you need:

**Hardware changes:**
1. Remove R17 and Q4 — eliminates the automatic but broken V50_I detection circuit
2. Wire ME2149 CE pin → GPIO 18 (via a 10kΩ pull-up to 3.3V as well) — the pull-up ensures ME2149 stays ON by default during boot before firmware takes control
3. Move ADS1115 channel 3 wire from V50 to V50_I (J1 pin 9) — now reads solar panel input directly: 5.5–6V when sun is present, 0V at night

**One flag on GPIO 18**: that pin is shared with the ultrasonic trigger. If TopTank ever uses sonar for level measurement, this conflicts. GPIO 32 was the cleaner choice for exactly this reason — it's free with no other assignment. Worth considering before you solder.

**Software logic (one wake cycle):**
```
On each wake:
  v50i = ADS1115 ch3 reading  (now V50_I)
  
  if v50i > 4.5V:              // solar is adequate
      GPIO18 = LOW             // ME2149 off → MCP73123 charges freely
  else:                        // night or cloudy
      GPIO18 = HIGH            // ME2149 on → battery boosts V50

  // ... sensors, LoRa TX, etc.
  
  Before goToSleep():
      // hold current GPIO18 state across sleep
      gpio_hold_en(GPIO_NUM_18)
```

**Threshold caveat**: 4.5V is a starting point. If V50_I drops to 4.5V under load (ESP32 + LoRa drawing from solar alone), you'd get V50 brownouts. You might need to tune this upward, or add hysteresis — disable ME2149 at >5.0V, re-enable at <4.0V.

**With the INA219 on Wally 16**: once that's in, replace the voltage threshold with current threshold (`solar_current > 50mA`). Current-based detection is more robust than voltage because a weak panel can still show 5V open-circuit but can't actually supply the load.

--- USER ---
my mistake is not 18 is 32 . actually i would imagine that putting a limit resistor in front of gpio18 before it goes into the CE pin would be a good idea no? so both pull up and limiting or just pulup?

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Good catch on GPIO 32. For the resistors — you want **both**, but they serve different purposes:

**Pull-up (10kΩ, 3.3V → CE):**  
Sets the default state. During boot before the firmware runs, GPIO 32 floats — the pull-up ensures CE stays HIGH so ME2149 stays ON and V50 is maintained. Without this, the device might not boot reliably.

**Series resistor (100Ω–330Ω, GPIO 32 → CE):**  
Protects the GPIO pin from transients. The ME2149 is a switching converter — its CE pin can see noise spikes from the switching. A small series resistor absorbs those before they reach the ESP32 GPIO. It also limits current if something goes wrong.

The key is keeping the series resistor **small**. It forms a voltage divider with the pull-up when GPIO is LOW:

- With 100Ω series + 10kΩ pull-up: CE voltage when GPIO=LOW = 3.3V × 100/10100 = **0.033V** → clean LOW ✓
- With 10kΩ series + 10kΩ pull-up: CE voltage when GPIO=LOW = **1.65V** → undefined, risky ✗

So: 10kΩ pull-up from 3.3V to CE, and 100Ω–330Ω in series from GPIO 32 to CE. That's the full circuit.

quick question, the MMBTRA104SS with jlcpcb part C51814210 what is the threshold voltage

--- CLAUDE ---

--- CLAUDE ---

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
It's not a MOSFET — it's a **PNP pre-biased bipolar transistor** (digital transistor). No Vgs threshold because it's not a MOSFET. Key specs:

- **Type**: PNP BJT, pre-biased
- **Built-in input resistor**: 47kΩ
- **Ic max**: 100mA
- **Package**: SOT-23

For switching purposes, it turns ON when the base is pulled roughly **0.7V below the emitter** (standard Vbe). With the internal 47kΩ input resistor, driving the base to GND from a 3.3V supply gives base current of 3.3V/47kΩ ≈ 70µA — enough to saturate it for the 100mA load.

Is this the transistor you're planning to use for the ME2149 CE control, or is it Q4 from the existing schematic you're removing? The reason it matters: a PNP here would mean GPIO drives base LOW to turn ON the transistor (and then transistor output controls CE), rather than GPIO driving CE directly. Want to walk through how you're using it?

Microchip is blocking direct PDF access. Based on what I know from the datasheet:

**MCP73123-22 key voltages:**

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Regulated charge voltage (-22 suffix) | **4.20V** ±0.75% |
| Input voltage range (VDD) | 3.75V – 6.0V |
| UVLO threshold (input) | ~3.6V |
| Preconditioning threshold | **3.15V** (75% of 4.2V — below this it trickle charges at ~10% current) |
| Charge termination | C/10 current cutoff |

**Important flag**: The MCP73123-22 is a Li-Ion charger targeting **4.2V**. Your LiFePO4 cell has a max charge voltage of **3.65V**. The charger is trying to push 4.2V into a cell rated for 3.65V max. If the cell has a built-in BMS it will protect itself by cutting off — but the charger never reaches its regulation voltage, so it stays in constant-current mode indefinitely and never terminates. This is likely contributing to your battery problems. The CN3801 in Rev 8 won't have this issue since you'll set it correctly for LiFePO4.

**On the MMBTRA104SS being Q4**: understood — it's the PNP switch being removed. Makes sense as the design: solar V50_I drove the base to switch ME2149 off, but as we identified, V50_I couldn't distinguish solar from ME2149's own output.

--- USER ---
i thought the CN3801 was native for lifepo4 what would i need to set it at?

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Let me check what we already documented for the Rev 8 CN3801 design.

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
The CN3801 (JLCPCB C840499) is the right call — it has the LiFePO4 regulation voltage **built in at 3.625V**, so there's nothing to configure for the battery voltage. That's already correct for LiFePO4 with no external resistors needed for that parameter.

What you do set externally:
- **RCS = 0.2Ω** → sets charge current to 600mA (1C for your 600mAh cell)
- **R1/R2 (270kΩ/100kΩ)** → sets MPPT input voltage to 4.46V (keeps V50 from sagging below what Wally's regulator needs)

The MCP73123-22 at 4.2V is wrong for LiFePO4 in both directions — too high for the cell chemistry and has no MPPT so it can't actively harvest solar. The CN3801 fixes both problems.

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
You're right, I was wrong. The MCP73123/223 is explicitly a **LiFePO4** charger. I confused it with Li-Ion variants. Apologies.

**Key voltages from the datasheet:**

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Regulated charge voltage (VREG) | **3.60V** (3.582–3.618V) ← correct for LiFePO4 |
| Minimum operating VDD | **4.2V** |
| **UVLO start threshold (VSTART)** | **4.15V typ** |
| **UVLO stop threshold (VSTOP)** | **4.05V typ** |
| UVLO hysteresis | 100mV |
| Preconditioning threshold (VPTH) | **2.0V** (below this → 10% trickle) |
| OVP threshold | 6.5V |
| Auto-recharge threshold | **95% × 3.6V = 3.42V** (for -22S variant) |

**The UVLO is critical for your circuit.** VDD (V50) must stay above **4.15V** for the charger to run. If ME2149 is running under load and V50 dips below 4.05V, the MCP73123 shuts off completely. That's a plausible contributing factor on top of the ME2149 competition — when the battery is depleted and ME2149 is struggling, V50 might be sagging enough to hit UVLO.

The charger itself is correct. The architecture of sharing V50 with ME2149 is the problem, not the component choice.

--- USER ---
ok so we left there that all i need to do is replace q4 with  the pullup circuit that has gpio32 through serial resusance and thatt it correct?

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
Almost — you also need the ADS1115 change, otherwise the firmware has no way to know if solar is actually present:

**Complete hardware changes:**

1. **Remove R17 and Q4** — eliminates the broken automatic detection circuit
2. **10kΩ pull-up** from 3.3V to ME2149 CE — keeps ME2149 ON during boot before firmware runs
3. **100Ω–330Ω series resistor** from GPIO 32 to ME2149 CE — GPIO protection
4. **Move ADS1115 ch3 wire** from V50 to V50_I (J1 pin 9) — without this, ADS1115 always sees 5V+ whether it's solar or ME2149, and the firmware can't tell the difference

Without #4, the software has no reliable signal to decide when to pull GPIO 32 LOW. You'd be forced to use time-only (sunrise/sunset) control with no fallback for cloudy days — which would brownout the device any time it's overcast and ME2149 is off.

With V50_I on ADS1115 ch3: V50_I = 0V at night, 5.5–6V in sun, somewhere in between on cloudy days. The firmware can threshold it and make a real decision.

So: Q4 out + R17 out + pull-up + series resistor + ADS1115 rewire. That's the complete set.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - Analyze TopTank battery data (72h and 8h windows) to understand charging/discharging behavior
   - Investigate why battery dies overnight despite sunny days
   - Understand root cause of ME2149/MCP73123 charging competition on shared V50 rail
   - Investigate why device was sleeping and not transmitting (boot loop due to depleted battery)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Teleonome API**: `http://chinampamonitor.local/TeleonomeServlet?formName=GetTelepathonRecordsForLastHours&telepathonName=TopTank&hours=N`
   - **INA219 sign convention**: negative current = charging (into battery), positive = discharging (from battery)
   - **ME2149 vs MCP73123 competition**: Both share V50 rail. ME2149 boosts battery→V50. MCP73123 charges battery from V50. When ME2149 is active, it holds V50 at regulation voltage so MCP73123 backs off → near-zero net charging
   - **Effective solar day bug**: PowerManager `calculateOptimalSleepTime()` uses `efficiency > 0.3` as daytime gate. At -37.13° lat in late June, efficiency crosses 0.3 only between ~09:30 and ~15:00 AEST. Outside that window, minutesToSunrise wraps to ~1380 min (past sunrise treated as next day), giving ~280s sleep instead of 60s
   - **LED brightness formula**: `isLoraTxSafe()` returns `calculateSafeLEDBrightness() * efficiency` → LEDs dim/off when efficiency low
   - **LED gate**: `efficiency * 100 > minimumEfficiencyForLed` (default 40 from NVS)
   - **TOTP authentication**: LoRa packets validated using time-based OTP. Year 2926 typo in SetTime caused TOTP to be 900 years wrong → all packets rejected
   - **TPL5010 watchdog**: Set for 15 minutes. Reboots device if DONE pin not toggled. Device in boot loop = wakes every 15 min, crashes before LoRa TX
   - **LiFePO4 flat discharge curve**: Voltage unreliable for SOC between 10-90%
   - **V50_I vs V50**: V50 is always 5V+ (from solar OR ME2149 output). V50_I comes only from solar panel header (J1 pin 9) → 0V at night, 5.5-6V in sun. Only V50_I can distinguish solar presence
   - **Dusk2Dawn DST bug**: `getDailySolarData()` calls `dusk2Dawn.sunrise(year, month, date, true)` with DST=true year-round, adding 1 hour to sunrise/sunset in winter. Firmware shows sunrise=08:33 instead of actual 07:32
   - **MCP73123 UVLO**: Charger shuts off if VDD < 4.05V (stop threshold). Must stay above 4.15V to operate
   - **CN3801 Rev 8**: MPPT charger, built-in 3.625V for LiFePO4, set RCS=0.2Ω for 600mA

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **SetTime year typo (2926 instead of 2026)**: First SetTime command sent as `27/6/2926 10:59:45`. TOTP codes were wrong by 900 years → all LoRa packets rejected by receivers. Fixed by second SetTime: `27/6/2026 11:5:55`. After correction, LoRa reception restored.
   - **Wrong assumption about MCP73123-22**: Initially stated it targets 4.2V (Li-Ion). User produced datasheet (DS22191E) proving it's a LiFePO4 charger targeting 3.60V. Corrected.
   - **INA219 bus voltage misread (0.75-0.98V)**: Initially thought this indicated wiring problem. User clarified battery was disconnected during office test (usingSolarPower=false). With no battery, INA219 sees floating/near-zero → readings are noise, not fault.
   - **V50 vs V50_I confusion**: Proposed using V50 threshold from ADS1115 to detect solar. User correctly pointed out V50 is always 5V+ from either solar or ME2149 — can't distinguish. Fix: change ADS1115 ch3 to read V50_I instead.
   - **Wrong GPIO selection**: Initially confirmed GPIO 18 for ME2149 CE control. User corrected to GPIO 32. GPIO 18 conflicts with ultrasonic trigger/flow meter.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Root cause of battery dying overnight**: ME2149 and MCP73123 compete on V50 rail. During day: near-zero net charging (ME2149 discharges at ~175mA, MCP73123 charges at ~174mA, net ≈ 0). During night: ME2149 drains battery for sleep load with no solar to compensate. Battery dies by morning.
   - **Boot loop diagnosis**: Battery depleted → ESP32 can't sustain startup current spike → brownout reset → TPL5010 retries every 15 min. LEDs flash briefly (boot visible), then crash. Solar provides 60mA insufficient for ESP32 startup peak.
   - **Effective solar day shrinkage**: PowerManager efficiency gate (>0.3) limits active operation to 09:30-15:00 AEST in Melbourne winter instead of 07:32-17:09. 5.5h effective day vs 9.5h actual daylight. Fix options: replace efficiency gate with actual sunrise/sunset check, or use BH1750 lux.
   - **TOTP failure**: Year 2926 typo in SetTime → wrong codes → packet rejection. Fixed with second SetTime.
   - **Hardware fix designed**: Remove Q4+R17, add GPIO32 control with pull-up + series resistor, rewire ADS1115 to V50_I.

## Build 8 Hardware Review

### What I found

**1. GPIO32 → ME2149 CE control (R17=10k pull-up, R18=330Ω series)**

Circuit math:
- GPIO32=LOW (day, ME2149 off): CE = 3.3V × 330/(10k+330) = **0.106V** → well below any CE high threshold ✓
- GPIO32=HIGH (night, ME2149 on): CE pulled to 3.3V by both resistors ✓
- Default at boot (GPIO32 floating/input): 10k pulls CE HIGH → ME2149 starts ON before firmware configures the pin — safe behaviour, device can run on battery through boot ✓

**2. ADS1115 → V50_I** — correct. V50_I is 0V at night, 5.5–6V from panel in sun. Now solar presence is actually detectable.

**3. D1 (LCSC C965793, 0402 LED) + R27 (1.2k) on STAT pin** — STAT is open-drain, pulls LOW while in CC charge phase. At V50=5V, Vf≈2V:
- I = (5−2)/1200 = **2.5mA** ✓ — within spec, good visible brightness

**4. Diode removed from MCP73123** — without seeing the Rev 7 schematic it's not clear which diode, but it was probably isolating VDD or VBAT. Either way the MCP73123 is a direct-connect battery charger so direct battery connection on output is fine per datasheet.

---

### One issue to be aware of: night circular drain

At night with GPIO32=HIGH (ME2149 ON):
- ME2149 boosts battery → V50 ≈ 5V
- MCP73123 sees VDD=5V with battery not full → charges at ICHG (whatever RPROG sets)
- Path: battery → ME2149 (90% eff) → V50 → MCP73123 linear (3.3/5 = 66% eff) → battery
- Net loop efficiency: 0.9 × 0.66 = **59%** → 41% waste as heat

The STAT LED will also glow at night during this circular charging (STAT pulled LOW by MCP73123). At 2.5mA that's ~20mAh over an 8h night = 3.3% of the 600mAh pack — minor but real.

This is a known limitation of MCP73123 having no disable pin. The Rev 8 design is still much better than Rev 7 (where ME2149 competed with MCP73123 all day long), because now ME2149 is only ON at night when there's no alternative. The CN3801 in Rev 9 eliminates this completely.

---

### Software tasks still needed before Rev 8 can fly

These were planned in the previous session but not yet implemented:

1. **GPIO32 control logic** — on each wake, read ADS1115 ch3 (V50_I):
   ```cpp
   float v50i = readADS1115_V(3);
   if (v50i > 4.5f) {
       digitalWrite(32, LOW);   // solar present → ME2149 off
   } else {
       digitalWrite(32, HIGH);  // no solar → ME2149 on
   }
   ```

2. **Hold GPIO32 across deep sleep** — before `esp_deep_sleep_start()`:
   ```cpp
   gpio_hold_en(GPIO_NUM_32);
   ```

3. **Fix calculateOptimalSleepTime() efficiency gate** — replace `efficiency > 0.3` with actual sunrise/sunset check so the device runs during the full solar day (currently limited to 09:30–15:00).

4. **Fix Dusk2Dawn DST=true always** — change `true` → `false` in `getDailySolarData()` so sunrise/sunset are reported correctly in winter.

Here's the complete CN3801 circuit based on the values we calculated. Note the CN3801 is a **buck** converter (like the CN3791 but for LiFePO4 at 3.625V), so the inductor is in the power path.

---

## CN3801 Circuit (JLCPCB C840499)

### Connection diagram

```
V50_I (from J1 pin 9) ───┬──────────────── VIN
(solar header, 4.7–5.2V) │                  │
                        C_IN             C_IN2
                       10µF/10V         100nF
                       0805 X5R         0402
                          │                │
                         GND             GND

VIN ─── R1 (270kΩ 0402) ─┬── MPPT pin (input voltage regulation FB)
                          │
                      R2 (100kΩ 0402)
                          │
                         GND

                SW ─── L1 (22µH, ≥1.5A sat) ─┬─── RCS (0.2Ω 0402) ─── BAT ──── Battery (+)
                                              │
                                           C_BAT
                                          10µF/10V
                                          0805 X5R
                                              │
                                             GND

              COM ─── R_COMP (120Ω 0402) ─── GND
               │
            C_COMP (220nF 0402)
               │
              GND

             TEMP ─── R_TEMP (10kΩ 0402) ─── GND
             (NTC not needed; 10k simulates 25°C, keeps charger always active)

               CE ─── V33  (or 3.3V from logic; HIGH = enabled)

             STAT ─── R (1.2kΩ) ─── LED (anode) ─── VIN
             (open-drain; LOW = charging in progress)
             (or STAT → LED → R → VIN, same result)
```

---

### Component list for BOM

| Ref | Value | Package | JLCPCB note |
|-----|-------|---------|-------------|
| U_CHG | CN3801 | SOP-8 | C840499 |
| L1 | 22µH ≥1.5A sat | 0805 or 1210 | pick separately |
| RCS | 0.2Ω (200mΩ) | 0402 1/4W | LVK12R... or similar |
| R1 | 270kΩ | 0402 | C25771 |
| R2 | 100kΩ | 0402 | C25803 |
| R_COMP | 120Ω | 0402 | C25279 |
| C_COMP | 220nF | 0402 | |
| R_TEMP | 10kΩ | 0402 | C25804 |
| C_IN | 10µF 10V X5R | 0805 | |
| C_IN2 | 100nF | 0402 | |
| C_BAT | 10µF 10V X5R | 0805 | |
| R_LED | 1.2kΩ | 0402 | C25862 (same as R27 on STAT) |

---

### Important layout notes

The SW→L1→RCS→BAT path switches at 300kHz — keep it as a tight loop, traces as short and wide as possible. C_IN goes right at the IC VIN/GND pins, not at the connector. GND fill under the IC for thermal.

**Do not reuse the ME2149 inductor** (3.3µH) — it will give 850mA ripple on 600mA average and saturate. You need 22µH.

---

### One thing to verify before laying out

The exact CN3801 pin numbering — download the datasheet from the JLCPCB C840499 page (Consonance Semiconductor). The pin *functions* above are correct; I want you to map them to the physical pin numbers in KiCad so the footprint is right.

## BQ24650 Circuit for LiFePO4 at 600mA

```
Solar input (V50_I) ──── ACIN ──┬── MPPSET divider (sets panel MPP voltage)
                                │   VIN ── R_M1 (240k) ──┬── MPPSET
                                │                         │
                                │                     R_M2 (100k)
                                │                         │
                                │                        GND
                                │   → VMPPT = 2.1 × (1 + 240/100) = 5.04V
                                │   → use 200k/100k for 4.5V: 2.1×(1+200/100) = 6.3V
                                │   → actually: VMPPT = 2.1×(1+R_M1/R_M2)
                                │   → for 4.5V: R_M1=114k, R_M2=100k  ← use these
                              C_IN
                             10µF/10V
                              0805
                                │
                               GND

BQ24650 switching output:
  HIDRV ── Gate of Q_HI (N-channel, e.g. SI2302, SOT-23)
  LODRV ── Gate of Q_LO (N-channel, same)
  BTST  ── 100nF bootstrap cap to SW node
  SW/PH ── source of Q_HI / drain of Q_LO ── L1 (22µH ≥1.5A) ── BAT output

BAT output ──┬── Battery (+)
             │
           C_BAT
          10µF/10V
           0805
             │
            GND

Charge voltage set (VFB pin):
  BAT ── R_V1 (560kΩ) ──┬── VFB
                         │
                     R_V2 (1MΩ)        → VBAT = 2.1 × (1 + R_V1/R_V2)
                         │              → 3.60V: R_V1=571k, R_V2=1M → use 560k/1M = 3.576V ✓
                        GND

Charge current set (RSNS, between GND and source of Q_LO):
  RSNS = 0.05Ω → ICHG = 0.05/RSNS ... wait, BQ24650: ICHG = 0.05/RSNS
  → RSNS = 0.05/0.6 = 83mΩ → use 82mΩ (2× 165mΩ in parallel, or LVK12R082)

REGN ── 4.7µF to GND  (internal LDO, must bypass)
STAT1, STAT2 ── each through 1kΩ to LED to GND  (open-drain status outputs)
CE ── V33 (enable)  or tie HIGH
TS ── 10kΩ to GND  (temp sense disable, same as TEMP on CN3801)
```

---

### External MOSFETs

Use two identical small N-channel FETs, e.g. **SI2302DS** (SOT-23, 20V, 3.6A, Vgs_th ≈1.5V). Both driven by BQ24650's built-in gate driver. High-side needs the bootstrap cap.

JLCPCB has SI2302 as C10487 (~$0.03 each).

---

### Compared to what you have now

| | MCP73123 (current Rev 8) | BQ24650 (Rev 9) |
|---|---|---|
| Topology | Linear | Synchronous buck |
| MPPT | No | Yes (VIN_DPM) |
| LiFePO4 | ✓ 3.6V | ✓ programmable |
| External FETs | None | 2× N-ch SOT-23 |
| BOM complexity | Low | Medium |

---

The Rev 8 board with the GPIO32/ME2149 fix is still a meaningful improvement and worth running — the MCP73123 will charge correctly now that ME2149 isn't competing during the day. BQ24650 is the right upgrade for Rev 9 PCB.

LT3652 is actually a better fit than BQ24650 for this application — it has an **integrated power switch**, so no external FETs. Simpler BOM than BQ24650.

## LT3652 for LiFePO4 at 600mA

**Key specs:**
- VIN: 4.95V to 32V (fine for V50_I at 5.5-6V from panel)
- Integrated P-channel high-side switch — no external FETs
- Programmable float voltage via resistor divider (set to 3.6V for LiFePO4)
- MPPT via VIN_REG pin (input voltage regulation)
- RSENSE sets charge current (100mV threshold)
- Package: MSOP-12 or DFN-12

---

### Circuit

```
V50_I ──────────────┬──── VIN
                  C_IN
                 10µF/10V
                  0805
                    │
                   GND

MPPT setpoint (VIN_REG pin):
  VIN ── R_M1 ──┬── VIN_REG
                │
             R_M2
                │
               GND
  VMPPT = 2.7V × (1 + R_M1/R_M2)  ← verify 2.7V ref in datasheet
  For 4.5V: R_M1=200kΩ, R_M2=300kΩ

SW ── L1 (22µH ≥1.5A) ──┬── SENSE+ ── RSENSE (165mΩ) ── SENSE- ── BAT+
                         │
                       C_BAT
                      10µF/10V
                       0805
                         │
                        GND
  ICHG = 100mV / RSENSE = 100mV / 0.165 = 606mA ✓

Float voltage (VBAT pin, battery sense):
  BAT+ ── R_V1 ──┬── VBAT
                 │
              R_V2
                 │
                GND
  VFLOAT formula is in datasheet — look up for 3.6V LiFePO4
  (reference is either 3.3V or 2.1V, changes the divider ratio)

BOOST ── 100nF ── SW  (bootstrap cap, mandatory)
TIMER ── 10µF to GND  (safety timeout — sets max charge duration)
SHDN  ── V33 or tie HIGH to enable
CHRG  ── 1kΩ ── LED ── GND  (open-drain, LOW = charging)
FAULT ── 1kΩ ── LED ── GND  (open-drain, LOW = fault)
```

---

### Values you need from the datasheet

Two things I'm not confident enough to give you without the actual LT3652 datasheet in front of me:

1. **VIN_REG reference voltage** — I believe 2.7V but verify. Changes R_M1/R_M2.
2. **VBAT reference voltage and divider formula** — this sets your 3.6V float. Critical to get right for LiFePO4.

The rest of the circuit (RSENSE, inductor, bootstrap, TIMER) I'm confident on.

---

### vs BQ24650

| | LT3652 | BQ24650 |
|---|---|---|
| External FETs | None (integrated) | 2× N-ch SOT-23 |
| BOM complexity | Lower | Higher |
| LiFePO4 | ✓ programmable | ✓ programmable |
| MPPT | ✓ VIN_REG | ✓ VIN_DPM |
| JLCPCB | Check — ADI/LT parts can be extended | C88306, available |
| Price | ~$1-2 | ~$0.50 |

**Check JLCPCB for the LT3652 first** — if it's available as a basic part it's the cleaner design. If it's not stocked or is extended-only with long lead time, BQ24650 is the fallback.

--- USER ---
found the C671115, now please explain why this ic does not have the 41% efficiency problem, how does it avoid the circular charging

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
The answer comes down to **where LT3652 gets its input** and **its minimum VIN**.

---

## Why LT3652 has no circular charging problem

### The MCP73123 problem (Rev 7/8)

MCP73123 is connected to **V50** — the shared bus that is always live. At night, ME2149 holds V50 at 5V from the battery. MCP73123 sees 5V → tries to charge → draws from V50 → which comes from ME2149 → which drains the battery it's trying to charge:

```
Battery → ME2149 (90%) → V50 5V → MCP73123 linear (66%) → Battery
                                                               ↑
                                         (5-3.6)/5 = 28% lost as heat
Net loop: 0.90 × 0.66 = 59% efficient → 41% wasted
```

MCP73123 has no way to know it's charging from itself.

---

### Why LT3652 is different

LT3652 connects to **V50_I** — the solar panel header pin directly. That net is:
- **Daytime:** 5.5–6V from panel ✓
- **Night:** **0V** — the panel produces nothing

LT3652 has a **minimum VIN of 4.95V**. At night V50_I = 0V → LT3652 is simply dead. No input, no operation, no circular loop. ME2149 can run all night and LT3652 never wakes up.

```
Night:
  Battery → ME2149 → V50 = 5V  (powers ESP32 load)
  V50_I = 0V  → LT3652 inactive  ← circular loop physically impossible

Day:
  Solar → V50_I = 5.5V  → LT3652 charges battery from panel
  ME2149 OFF (GPIO32 low)  → no battery discharge during charging
```

---

### The MPPT bonus

On top of that, MPPT means LT3652 **regulates how hard it pulls from the panel**. If the panel starts to sag (too cloudy, too much load), LT3652 backs off its charge current to hold the panel at its MPP voltage (4.5V). MCP73123 has no such control — it just pulls whatever it needs and collapses the panel voltage.

---

### Summary

| | MCP73123 | LT3652 |
|---|---|---|
| Input | V50 (always live) | V50_I (0V at night) |
| Active at night? | Yes — circular drain | No — can't start |
| Circular loop possible? | Yes, 41% loss | No |
| Panel voltage regulation | None | Yes (MPPT) |

The fix isn't software, it's topology: **connect the charger input to V50_I, not V50**. LT3652 only operates when the sun is up.

--- USER ---
actually the MCP73123 connects to the v5_i in the new design

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
That changes everything — if MCP73123 is already on V50_I in Rev 8, it already solves the circular charging problem by the same mechanism. V50_I = 0V at night → MCP73123 VDD drops below UVLO (4.05V) → charger goes inactive. No loop.

So the circular drain issue is **already fixed in Rev 8** just by moving to V50_I.

---

The remaining reasons to replace MCP73123 with LT3652 would be:

**1. Efficiency / heat**
MCP73123 is linear. At V50_I = 5.5V and VBAT = 3.3V it dissipates (5.5−3.3) × 0.6 = **1.32W as heat**. On a small PCB that's significant. LT3652 buck at 90% efficiency dissipates ~0.24W for the same charge current.

**2. MPPT for panel utilisation**
Without MPPT, MCP73123 and the ESP32 load both pull from V50_I and the panel finds its own operating point. On a weak or partially shaded day, the panel voltage can sag below what MCP73123 needs and charge current drops. LT3652 actively holds the panel at its MPP voltage (4.5V), so it extracts full available power even in poor conditions.

**3. Faster charging on a short solar day**
At the Melbourne latitude with the effective solar day currently limited to ~5.5 hours, efficient use of every minute of sun matters. MPPT extracts more energy across that window.

---

But to be direct: **Rev 8 with MCP73123 on V50_I is already a fundamentally sound design**. It charges cleanly from solar, doesn't compete with ME2149 at night, and the LiFePO4 voltage is correct. LT3652 is an optimisation for Rev 9 — not a fix for a broken circuit.

--- USER ---
the issue is that the panel i am using for this product is the panel but it has a regulator already so that it ends up in usb

--- CLAUDE ---


--- CLAUDE ---
That's an important detail. If the panel has a built-in 5V USB regulator, the **MPPT advantage of LT3652 disappears** — you're already receiving a regulated 5V, not a raw panel with variable MPP characteristics. The panel's own circuitry handles that.

So what's left to compare:

---

### With a USB-regulated panel input

| | MCP73123 (Rev 8) | LT3652 |
|---|---|---|
| MPPT benefit | ✗ irrelevant — already 5V in | ✗ irrelevant |
| Circular charging | ✓ already solved (V50_I = 0V at night) | ✓ same |
| Efficiency | Linear: (5−3.6)×0.6 = **840mW heat** | Buck: ~90%, dissipates **~240mW** |
| Thermal throttling | MCP73123 has thermal foldback — reduces charge current when hot | No foldback needed |
| Complexity | Already on the board | Needs L1, redesign |

---

The only remaining argument for LT3652 is **heat**. 840mW dissipated continuously in a field enclosure that can reach 50°C ambient is uncomfortable — MCP73123 will thermally foldback and charge slower than it should.

But honestly: **Rev 8 with MCP73123 on V50_I is already correct for this panel**. The big problems (circular charging, ME2149 competing during day) are fixed. The thermal throttling issue is real but manageable.

LT3652 becomes worthwhile only if you switch to a **raw panel without a built-in regulator** in the future — that's when MPPT + efficiency together make a meaningful difference.

What panel is it? Knowing the USB output current rating tells you whether 600mA charge + 70mA ESP32 stays within what the panel's regulator can supply.
